3
WESTPAC ECONOMICS  
Savings growth by mortgage status
Households continue to rebuild savings
Savings increased 1.3% in Q3, with the strongest gains again 
recorded among younger households. Savings balances for 
18–34 year olds increased 2.0%qtr, compared with 0.7% 
for those aged 55 and over. Mortgagors also continued to 
accumulate savings more rapidly, recording a 2.8% increase 
compared with 0.7% among non-mortgagors. Savings growth 
among mortgagors has now outpaced non-mortgagors for 
three consecutive years.
Stronger savings have continued to lift mortgage buffers. 
Average buffers (savings divided by the sum of essential 
expenses and minimum mortgage repayments), increased to 
22.2 months of repayments in September. Looking within the 
unit-record sample, median mortgage buffers jumped from 
6.3 months in August to 6.8 months, its highest level since the 
series began in 2019.

Median mortgage buffers
Fuel spending a clear drag on consumers
Fuel spending as a share of total spending
Spending outcomes continued to vary across age groups. 
The squeeze remains most apparent among younger 
households. Fuel spending rose 5.1%qtr for 18–34 year olds, 
against a 1.5%qtr increase in non-fuel spending. By contrast, 
non-fuel spending increased 2.1%qtr among households aged 
55 and over. 
The gap between mortgagors and non-mortgagors was 
more modest. Spending rose 1.6%qtr among mortgagors 
and 1.9%qtr among those without a mortgage, suggesting 
mortgage status alone masks considerable variation in 
household financial positions – a divide that becomes clearer 
when households are grouped by the size of their mortgage 
buffers (see discussion further on in this note). Note that ‘non 
mortgagors’ covers a diverse range of consumers including 
those that are renting and those that own their homes without 
a mortgage.

### Page 4

Past performance is not a reliable indicator of future performance. The forecasts given above are predictive in character. Whilst every effort has been taken to ensure that the assumptions on which the forecasts are based are reasonable, 
the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
4
WESTPAC ECONOMICS  
Median litres of fuel consumed
More importantly, their consumption appears to be less 
responsive to price movements. During periods of cheaper 
fuel, high-buffer households show a noticeably larger lift in 
litres purchased, consistent with stockpiling when prices are 
favourable. Low-buffer households show much less of this 
response, while their fuel volumes have also adjusted less 
during the recent increase in fuel costs.
The spending data show the consequence. Fuel expenditure 
has risen sharply across all three groups recently, but remains 
highest among low-buffer households, where it also absorbs 
a larger share of total spending. Food spending has also 
declined since 2023. This may partly reflect households 
economising within the category, including switching towards 
cheaper or home-brand products, although further work is 
needed to test this.
Taken together, the households with the smallest savings 
cushions face higher mortgage repayments, spend more 
on fuel and appear to have less scope to adjust their fuel 
consumption when prices change. 
Discretionary spend as a share of total
This suggests that in aggregate, household balance sheets 
are looking healthy. The picture is a little at odds with the very 
weak reads on our Westpac-Melbourne Index Consumer 
Sentiment Index which suggests finances are coming under 
much more pressure. The fact that both average spending and 
saving both rose suggests incomes over the quarter were not 
overly stretched and that more of the weakness in sentiment 
may relate to concerns about the outlook (something that may 
also be encouraging consumers to build buffers now, while they 
still can).
Pressures intense for low-buffer households
Aggregate buffer measures mask sizeable differences across 
mortgagors. We can get a sense of these variations by 
combining mortgagors into three broad groups according to 
buffer size, i.e. low, middle and high buffer groups. This shows 
that households with the smallest buffers are also more 
exposed to both interest rates and fuel costs.

The buffer differences are relatively stark, the median savings 
of the low-buffer group just $2k compared to $15k for the 
middle-buffer group and $95k for the high buffer group. 
Low buffer households face median minimum mortgage 
repayments of around $2,600 a month, compared with $2,400 
for the middle buffer group and $2,200 for the highest-buffer 
group. 
Low buffer households also spend more on fuel, at around 
$170 a month compared with $160 for the middle buffer 
group and $150 for the high buffer group.

We can use average pump prices to estimate what this 
expenditure implies for fuel volumes. Low-buffer households 
consume around 70 litres a month, middle-buffer households 
67 litres a month and the high-buffer group around 62 litres. 
This compares to an average of 81, 80 and 70 respectively 
through 2024-25. 
Median fuel spend by customer

### Page 5

Past performance is not a reliable indicator of future performance. The forecasts given above are predictive in character. Whilst every effort has been taken to ensure that the assumptions on which the forecasts are based are reasonable, 
the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
5
WESTPAC ECONOMICS  
Yet despite this greater exposure, the gap in broader spending 
behaviour has narrowed. High-buffer households continue 
to spend more on discretionary categories, averaging around 
$1,940 a month compared with $1,740 for low-buffer 
households. However, their discretionary share has been 
broadly stable since 2023, while the shares of low- and 
middle-buffer households have risen by close to a percentage 
point bringing them more in line with the high-buffer group.

Some of the convergence reflects a longer run down-trend in 
fuel spending that has been more pronounced amongst low 
and middle buffer households. This lowering in fuel spend lifts 
the share available for other categories.

Essential spending excluding fuel, meanwhile, has changed 
little across the three cohorts since 2023. The shift in 
spending patterns has largely occurred on the discretionary 
side.

The result is a somewhat mixed picture. Low-buffer 
households remain considerably more exposed to mortgage 
and fuel costs and have far smaller savings cushions. But their 
discretionary spending has nevertheless moved closer to that 
of better-buffered households, suggesting some recovery in 
spending flexibility despite these constraints. 
Conclusion
Aggregate household finances remain resilient: spending and 
savings both increased in Q3 and mortgage buffers continued 
to rebuild. Discretionary spending has also held up despite the 
sharp increase in fuel costs.

But aggregate resilience masks a much more uneven 
distribution of pressure. Low-buffer households 
simultaneously face higher mortgage repayments, greater fuel 
exposure and much smaller savings cushions. While these 
households have so far maintained discretionary spending, 
their capacity to absorb further pressure is clearly more 
limited.
About the Westpac-DataX Consumer Panel
Developed by DataX, Westpac’s data analytics service for 
institutional and business banking customers, the dataset links 
transaction activity with balance sheet information to give 
a complete view of income, spending, saving and borrowing 
flows. 
The resulting sample of over one million customers provides 
an accurate representation of trends across the wider 
Australian consumer. Monthly observations are available back 
to January 2019 with timely updates provided a few weeks 
after the end of each month. As such, it is perfectly suited to 
quickly tracing the impact and responses to events like interest 
rate moves and energy price shocks.

October 2026
First Home
Buyer Report

### Page 2

2 © 2026 CoreLogic NZ Limited t/as Cotality. Proprietary
Cotality’s
First Home Buyer
(FHB) Report has tracked
purchasing activity for
several years,
analysing
the
types of properties bought,
the prices paid, and broader
shifts in entry
-
level demand.
The latest edition updates
these measures and extends
the analysis to examine FHB
activity across different
price brackets.
Now in its fourth edition in
partnership with Westpac,
the report also draws on the

bank’s mortgage lending
data to provide additional
insight into borrower
characteristics. That
includes the average age of
FHB borrowers, a measure
not widely available
elsewhere, as well as loan
-
to
-
value ratios across new
mortgages.
For clarity, we define a first home
buyer purchase to be where no party
involved has owned property in New
Zealand before and are using a
mortgage to make the deal.
Note:
The Westpac FHB age and LVR data
are sourced from their lending records

and is not matched to the Cotality
database for this report.
Cotality – Westpac
First Home Buyer Report

### Page 3

3
© 2026 CoreLogic NZ Limited t/as Cotality. Proprietary
FHBs’ market share remains
at record high levels.
In Q3
2026, they accounted for
29.5% of property purchases
across NZ, surpassing the
previous mark of 28.3% only
set three months earlier. The
number of deals remains at
historically high levels too,
bucking the wider trend of
slowing sales volumes for
groups such as investors and
relocating owner
-
occupiers.
This strength for FHBs is
being seen right across the

country,
with market shares
generally running above their
own long
-
term averages
(since 2005). The wider
Wellington area remains a
hotspot for FHBs, accounting
for 37% of activity so far in
2026. But FHBs have also
been at least 30% of the
market in Hamilton and
Auckland, as well as Gisborne,
Rotorua, Hastings,
Palmerston North, and
Invercargill
–
South Waikato
and Waikato District too.
This high level of activity
hasn’t meant too many
compromises either;

standalone houses have
comprised 76% of all FHB
purchases so far in 2026, up
from 75% last year, and the
highest figure since 2020
(78%). To be fair, FHBs have
had to pay a little more, with a
median price so far this year
of $715,000, but still less than
nearly $719,000 in 2022.
FHBs are more active across
the value spectrum too.
For
example, in the middle 40% of
the market by value, their
share rose from 21% in 2015 to
28% in 2025, and now about

30% so far this year.
Access to low deposit
finance under the LVR rules
remains a key support for
first home buyers.
Recent
Reserve Bank data shows that
more than half of FHB loans
are done at less than 20%
deposit (or above 80% LVR),
and Westpac’s own lending
records show a recent
average LVR for FHBs of a
touch more than 80%.
The average age for FHBs on
Westpac’s books has ticked
higher lately, from around 35
late last year to about 35.5
now.
But rather than

necessity due to affordability
pressures, this is more likely
to reflect a choice from many
buyers to just wait for the
right property without fear the
market will run away again in
the meantime.
Looking ahead, although it’s
never easy to get that first
home, conditions should
remain
favourable
for FHBs.
Mortgage rates remain lower
than at many points in the
past, KiwiSaver access for at
least part of the deposit
remains a strong support, and
the banks remain willing and

able to lend at low
deposit/high LVR too.
This is clearly a ‘good
new
s
story’ which we should never
get blasé or complacent
about,
after so many previous
years (even decades) of
intense housing affordability
pressures and a declining
home ownership rate.
As we head into November’s
election, the bottom line is
that first home buyers are
starting from a strong base.
Key findings
Cotality – Westpac
First Home Buyer Report

### Page 4

4
© 2026 CoreLogic NZ Limited t/as Cotality. Proprietary
It’s been another solid few months
for first home buyers with their share
of house sales remaining around
record highs across the country.
That’s a stark contrast to the
softness in the broader housing
market over the past year.
Helping to support those high levels
of first home buyers have been a
couple of big trends. The first is the
large number of properties available
for sale. That’s meant those looking

to get their foot on the property
ladder have had plenty of options. As
a result, first home buyers have often
been able to get into newer and
larger standalone homes without
having to pay significantly more.
The other factor that has boosted
first home buyer numbers has been
the relatively low level of mortgage
rates in recent years. Combined with
broadly flat house prices, that’s
meant servicing a mortgage has
been much more affordable, with the

share of disposable income being
spen
t
on debt
-
servicing costs back
around pre
-
pandemic averages. For
the average first home buyer, lower
mortgage interest rates in recent
years shaved around $560 off their
monthly mortgage payments
compared to 2023 when interest
rates were at their peak. And in
Auckland, where first homes tend to
be more expensive, the saving is
around $685 a month.
While those are encouraging trends
for first home buyers, there are some

big changes in economic conditions
occurring which are likely to have
important implications for the
housing market over the year ahead.
Most notable has been the sharp rise
in inflation in the wake of the Middle
East war, with oil prices rocketing
higher since the start of this year.
That contributed to consumer prices
rising by 3.7% in the year to
September. Relatively high core
inflation levels mean we expect
inflation will push above 4% before

the end of this year and remain high
for much of 2027.
In response to those mounting
inflation pressures, and concerns
that they will persist for some time,
the RBNZ began lifting the Official
Cash Rate (OCR) back in July. We
expect further hikes in coming
months. The resulting pressure on
borrowing costs has seen mortgage
rates push higher in recent months,
with one
-
and two
-
year fixed rates
up around 0.75 percentage points
over the past year.

Even with the recent rise in
mortgage rates, the housing market
is still a lot more accessible for first
home buyers than a few years ago.
But with growing upward pressure
on interest rates, it’s a good time for
prospective first home buyers (or
anyone with a re
-
fix coming up) to
think about the best way to structure
their borrowing. That could help
insulate them from the likely trend
higher in the OCR over the next
couple of years and could save them

a lot of money over the life of their
mortgage. There’s a relatively
modest difference between short
-
and longer
-
term fixed mortgage
rates right now, which means the
cost of taking this ‘insurance’ is not
prohibitive.

not be shifting sharply higher any
time soon.

© 2026 CoreLogic NZ Limited t/as Cotality. Proprietary
The national picture
Since our previous FHB Report
back in May 2026, the wider
housing market has remained
subdued and patchy
–
generally
sales volumes have been flat to
falling, while property values have
been showing a similarly soft
picture, albeit not everywhere.
Amongst the main
centres
,
Auckland and Wellington, as
examples, have been more sluggish
than say Christchurch or Dunedin.
It’s also noteworthy that the stock

of listings available for sale remains
elevated across the country.
It’s not difficult to find the reasons
why the market remains in the
doldrums.
The economic backdrop
is still uncertain, the unemployment
rate is above average, and
mortgage rates have been drifting
higher in recent months as inflation
fears remain strong. A better
balance of physical housing supply
versus our population is also
dampening prices, while the
election has been a recent reason

for caution too. It’s harder to
measure, but ‘animal spirits’ aren’t a
key factor
at the moment
either.
FHBs have gone from strength to
strength
In this purchaser
-
friendly
environment, first home buyers
have continued to take full
advantage.
The Cotality Buyer
Classification data shows that their
market share hit a new quarterly
record high of 28.3% in Q2 2026,
only to push higher still in Q3
–
reaching 29.5%. They’re also faring
well in terms of the number of deals

too, recently hovering at around
25,000 on a 12
-
month rolling basis,
back to levels last seen (for a brief
period) at the height of the post
-
COVID boom.
Clearly, lower property values
–
still down by more than 18%
nationally from the peak
–
and
improved housing affordability
(even despite some recent rises in
mortgage rates) are helping FHBs.
But those aren’t the only factors;
access to (ever
-
growing) KiwiSaver
pots for at least part of the deposit

is also a factor, alongside decent
availability of high LVR finance at
the banks.
Indeed, the 25% allowance/speed
limit to lend to owner
-
occupiers at
less than 20% equity (or >80% LVR)
is not being tested
at the moment
and recently more than half of FHB
loans have been advanced at less
than 20% deposit.
Westpac’s own
mortgage lending records show
this in a different way
–
with the
average LVR for their loans to
FHBs in recent months still
running a touch above 80%.

LVRs
tend to be higher in Auckland, but a
bit lower in Canterbury and
Wellington.
They’re also getting more for their
money
FHBs have always tended to be
willing to compromise on location
or property type to make the shift
from renting into ownership, but
lately there hasn’t been much need
to take such an approach.
Over
2026 to date (Q3), standalone
houses have comprised 76% of all
FHB purchases, up from 75% last
year, and well above 2023’s
cyclical trough of less than 70%.

For comparison, standalone houses
have accounted for around 72% of
deals across all
buyers
year to date.
N
ational
FHB activity
Source: Cotality

First Home Buyer Report

### Page 8

8
      © 2026 CoreLogic NZ Limited t/as Cotality. Proprietary
This shows that FHBs are getting 
the most for their money in a 
quieter market. Admittedly, the 
median price they’ve paid so far 
this year has risen to $715,000, up 
from $701,000 in 2025 
 and also  
higher than the 2023 ($695,000) 
and 2024 figures ($692,000). But 
that latest price is still around 
$4,000 lower than in 2022, yet 
FHBs are still getting ‘more house’.
Another important point to make is 
that FHBs don’t always enter at the 
bottom of the housing ladder and 
work their way up 
 – 
many 
 actually 
enter  
well above the lowest tiers of 
the market. Indeed, the FHB 
median price paid of $715,000 this 
year is below the all
 -
 buyer figure of 
$785,000, but well above the all
 -
buyer lower quartile (bottom 25%) 
of $595,000. 
This point can be made another 
way too, with Cotality figures 
showing a rising share of activity 
for FHBs right across the price 
spectrum. Perhaps most notably, 
FHBs have accounted for 30% of 
purchases in the middle 40% price 
bracket of transactions so far this 
year, up from a 28% share in 2025, 
25% in 2020, and only 21% in 2015. 
They’ve even accounted for 15% of 
activity so far this year in the top 
30% of deals split by price.
FHBs’ average age has edged 
higher again, but probably just due 
to a lack of pressure
Meanwhile, at face value, 
Westpac’s data showing that the 
average age of 
 a 
FHB has recently 
edged back up to around 2024 
levels of 35.5 again, after a trough 
of 35 late last year, could seem at 
odds with the other, more 
encouraging trends. But rather than 
being out of necessity (
 e.g.  
due to 
affordability pressures), this could 
reflect the abundance of listings 
and subdued house prices, 
meaning that 
 some/many FHBs 
might just be choosing to wait a 
bit longer to get their ideal 
property, without fear they’ll be 
left behind by a rising market.

A solid outlook
Overall, it’s never easy to buy that 
first home and FHBs certainly 
continue to face challenges. But 
their presence has remained strong 
in recent months and, although 
nothing lasts forever, it’s not hard 
to imagine that FHBs stay a solid 
presence into 2027 as well. This is 
a ‘good 
 news 
 story
 ’ which we 
should never get 
 blasé 
 or 
complacent about, after so many 
previous years (decades) of intense 
housing affordability pressures and 
a declining home ownership rate.
Purchase price paid

The main centres
Looking at the data across
Auckland, Hamilton, Tauranga,
wider Wellington (i.e. combined
City, Lower Hutt, Upper Hutt,
Porirua), Christchurch, and
Dunedin, the national trends can
also be seen at the more granular
level
–
indeed, all of these areas
have had FHB market shares lately
that are above their own averages.
The wider Wellington area remains
a standout for FHBs, where their

share so far this year has been a
touch above 37%, well
in excess of
their long
-
term average in that
market of 30%. FHBs have also had
a market share in 2026 above 30%
in Hamilton and Auckland. The
figures have been a little lower in
Christchurch and Dunedin (both
around 28%), as well as Tauranga
(circa 25%), but in each of those
markets the figures have still been
4
-
8%
-
points above their own
averages.
Looking at the different property
types, standalone houses still

dominate in most areas, accounting
for 85
-
90% of FHB purchases in
Dunedin, Tauranga, and Hamilton
–
broadly in line with the
long term
norms in those areas. The share for
houses amongst FHB purchases is
always lower in Christchurch,
Wellington, and Auckland, but it’s
perhaps the former that stands out
most at present
–
with only 66% of
FHB purchases so far in 2026 in
Christchurch being standalone
houses, versus the historical
average of 75%. This no doubt

reflects the recent townhouse
construction upswing.
In each main
centre
, the median
FHB price is lower than the figure
for all buyers, but higher than the
lower quartile for all buyers
–
in
other words, FHBs don’t always
enter at the bottom. In terms of the
prices themselves, Auckland is the
main
centre
where FHBs pay the
most, at a median of $880,000 so
far in 2026, which is $110,000 less
than the all
-
buyer median, but
$110,000 more than the all

-
buyer
lower quartile. Median FHB prices
paid are $795,500 in Tauranga so
far in 2026, followed by Wellington
at $736,250, Hamilton $715,000,
and lower still in Christchurch and
Dunedin.

The main urban areas
Digging deeper into the next 12 key
centres
around New Zealand, the
story of relative strength for FHBs’
market share has remained in place
so far in 2026. Palmerston North
stands out with 33% of
transactions in the year to date
going to FHBs (6%
-
points above its
average), although Rotorua,
Gisborne, Hastings, and Invercargill
have also had FHB shares of 30%
or more this year.  Relative to their

own averages, Napier and Hastings
have been strongest, with figures
9%
-
points above normal.
But even in areas where FHBs have
traditionally found conditions
harder or have simply been less
interested, there’s still been relative
strength this year. For example,
Whangarei’s share going to FHBs
has been 25% in 2026 (versus
average since 2005 of 19%), Kapiti
Coast at 21% versus 17%, and
Queenstown
-
Lakes at 19% versus
15%.
Standalone houses tend to be the

dominant type of property amongst
the existing dwelling stock in many
provincial areas, and therefore also
account for a high share of FHB
purchases. So far this year, houses
have been at least 90% of FHB
purchases in Invercargill,
Palmerston North, Whanganui,
Rotorua, and Gisborne. It’s been
80
-
89% in Nelson, Kapiti Coast,
Hastings, Whangarei, Napier, and
New Plymouth. Queenstown is
lower at just 64%, but that’s likely
to reflect a wider range of property

types amongst the existing stock of
dwellings, as well as affordability
pressures which are probably
pushing FHBs towards lower price
brackets (and hence smaller
dwellings).
Indeed, in terms of median prices
paid by FHBs so far this year, the
highest figure (amongst the main
urban areas) has been in
Queenstown, at $1,275,000. Kapiti
Coast comes in at $700,000, with
Whangarei, Rotorua, Napier,
Hastings, New Plymouth, and
Nelson all at least $600,000. By

contrast, Invercargill ($480,000)
and Whanganui ($460,000) are at
the lower end.

The provinces
Around NZ’s provincial markets
(with a minimum number of 125
FHB purchases so far in 2026), all
have had
a
FHB % market share
above the long
-
term average. The
highest raw numbers have been in
South Waikato (34%), Waikato
District (31%), along with Ashburton
(28%), Timaru and Masterton (27%
apiece), and Selwyn (26%). The
figures have been less than 20% in
Far North, Western Bay of Plenty,

and Waimakariri. Compared to their
own averages, South Waikato and
Waikato District have also been
robust for FHBs, along with Selwyn
and Horowhenua.
Meanwhile, in terms of the prices
being paid by FHBs in these
provincial areas, Western Bay of
Plenty has had the highest figure so
far in 2026 ($760,000), with
Selwyn at $749,000, and Waipa
and Waikato District both on
$730,000. By contrast, the prices
have been $500,000 or less in
Masterton, Timaru, Horowhenua,

and South Waikato.

► 美伊外交持續斡旋 ，卡達與巴基斯坦提出折
衷方 案， 一度 增 添 市場對 兩 方談 判突 破預
期，然雙方對 核問題 意見仍分歧 ，疊加美以
雙方準備進行軍事備戰 ，恐於期中選後加大
對伊朗打擊 ，加劇中東局勢不確定性與市場
擾動。市場持續聚焦美國企業 財報季表現 ，
金融業財 報良好開局形成 股價下方支撐 ，而
賽默飛世爾 科技等醫療企業營收 、獲利與展

望皆高於預期，推動醫療類股漲勢。
► CoreWeave融資成本續升加劇 AI產業擔憂 ，
半導體與科技類股表現顯弱 。AI巨頭財報由
Meta平台打頭陣 ，AI驅動廣告 效率與營收與
展望 優 於 預期 並 上調全 年 資 本支 出 下 緣。
SpaceX獲利由虧轉盈且優於市場共識為股價
添柴火，Tesla財報好壞參半，短期內飛天跑
車貢獻不大 ，後續聚焦潛在合併所面臨的地
緣風險。通訊企業財報方面，儘管AT&T與網
飛財報優於預期 ，但面對衛星通訊 競爭疑慮

► 特斯拉3Q26財報好壞參半 — 毛利率與交付
量超預 期 ， 營收 大 致符合 ，營業 利潤 遜預
期，最大意外是自由現金流達 15億美元，高
於共識負43億，資本開支指引下調成憂慮。
► 早前公司展示下代 Roadster能力，本質上是
光環車 ，定價高受眾少 ，2028 年才有望投
產，盈利貢獻有限 。Model 3/Y成為大眾商
品，競爭激烈 ，利潤率壓縮 。正如傳統品牌
以賽道或參賽展示技術 ，「飛天」旨在展示
獨有跨界整合能力，維持品牌形象溢價。
► 至於和SpaceX的潛在合併，核心在於貫通多
頂 業 務， 提 高 資 本 運 用 效 率， 共同分擔
Terafab 的開支等 ，現時兩家合計市值約為
3.5兆美元。這項巨型潛在交易 ，除了要跨過
極高表決門檻外，SpaceX具極敏感的美國國
防及NASA合作背景 ，而特斯拉有超過 20%
業務倚重上海超級工廠 ，合併存在嚴重地緣
衝突。預期馬斯克將在川普離任前 ，加速執
行Tesla與SpaceX 合併。

► 今年9月適逢地緣擾動持續推升高油價、聯準
會重啟 升息 、財政部 與企 業發債 浪潮 等 影
響，形塑債市今年以來單月最差表現 。檢視
2021年以來美國投資級債前十大單月最差總
報酬，2022年聯準會 3月啟動升息年度就佔
一半以上，顯示利率風險仍為近5年來債市最
大風險 ，然檢視單月最差報酬表現未來期間
總報酬，除2022年1月和3月聯準會重啟升息
前夕與當月表現明顯較弱，多數於未來1年後
多呈現正報酬 ，反映隨利率走升放大孳息效
益，同時挹注債券長期持有之報酬空間。
► 今年來債券市場利率波動明顯推升信用債利
差，其 中僅 以低 信 評債利 差升幅 較顯 ，此
外，9月以來利率連動度高及AI相關產業債種
也成為利差擴大重災區 ，凸顯債市投資人減
輕市場不確定性的投資傾向 ，考量信用債市
歷經利率上行利空洗禮 ，信用利差同步擴大

創造收益空間 ，可把握債信體質仍佳企業債
鎖利良機，並透過時間複利力量強化收益。
利率波動升，僅低信評利差擴大程度相對明顯9月為美國投資級債今年最差單月報酬
用時間換空間，單月最差表現形塑未來報酬機會

### Page 8

► 受聯準會重啟升息影響 ，過去一年美國公債
殖利率曲線持續陡峭化 ，其中利率最大升幅
落在短天期的 2至3年期間，美國核心通膨與
就業仍具韌性 ，聯準會將持續保持 緊縮貨幣
政策步調來應對高通膨 ，考量美國高物價與
高利率環境短期 難消，宜透過縮短債券配置
天期並進行動態調整 ，為因應後續利率不確
定性環境的良策。
► 為因應利率不確定性 ，投資人可採取債券梯
的投資配置 ，主要選取不同到期期間及不同
類型債劵進行 組合，債券梯投資重點主要在
於，根據殖利率曲線型態及利率動態建立不
同期間 債券梯，假設市場利率走升且殖利率
曲線正斜率 ，建立短天期債券梯除了降低利
率風險 ，有 短期 閒 置資金 及孳息 需求 投資
人，亦可取得穩定孳息 收益。若後續投資人
有短期資金需求 ，可以視情況將短期債券到
期後資金回收 ，降低只投資單一債券須持至
到期或直接變現的流動性風險。
投資焦點
美國公債殖利率曲線持續為正斜率
高利率波動持續，用策略迎戰市場擾動
資料來源：Bloomberg，凱基證券整理

建立短天期債券梯，分散風險及穩定現金流
債券梯
經過第2年第一檔 債券到期，視資金需求 及市況與利率 動
態決定後續配置

短天期債券梯 ：假設買入 2檔各 3、4、5及7年到期債券 ，
聚焦 短期穩定收益
► 債券梯是一種分散投資方式 ，主要選取不同到期期間及不同
類型債劵進行組合，可兼具：
1) 分散利率風險： 將資金分別買進不同到期年期的債券 ，以分
散利率風險。
2) 分散信用風險： 分散投資不同發行 機構債券，不會重壓 單一
債券並可分散信用違約風險。
3) 降低流動性風險： 若投資人有短期資金需求 ，可以視情況將
短期債券到期後資金回收 ，降低只投資單一債券須持至到期
或直接變現之流動性風險。

### Page 9

股市
 美股第三季獲利年增率預估為雙位數成長 ，市場靜待財報結果與展望 ，和對AI資本支出回收影響 。
考量地緣衝突 、殖利率偏高、評價面壓力與鄰近美國期中選舉 ，短期金融市場紛擾不止 ，可增加防
禦股增強耐震度 。長期AI、科技發展趨勢不變 ，逢股市拉回時可分批布局 ，分散配置於不同國家與
產業，降低市場整體波動，亦可以槓鈴策略(防禦+成長)穩中求利。
 歐、日股可作為分散配置重點 ，全球利率走揚有利銀行股 。此外，地緣衝突頻傳 ，歐洲國防軍工仍
有投資題材。
策略風格：可增加防禦股配置，中長期看
好AI題材如科技及半導體，非AI部分關注
醫療保健生技產業
區域：日本銀行股、歐洲國防
債市
 全球公債殖利率攀升 ，預估今年底前主要國家仍有升息空間 ，殖利率偏高環境 使中短天期債券提供
吸引力，逢利率走高、鎖利領息。高信評債體質較佳 ，具穩定現金流與還款能力 ，聚焦風險調整後
利差具吸引力者，包含金融、通訊及公用事業等產業。
債種：中短天期高信評債鎖利，產業如金
融、通訊及公用事業等

匯市
 歐洲政治風險上升、歐元短期弱盤，美元高檔有撐。
 日本銀行再度升息時間不定，日圓區間震盪。
美元高檔有撐
商品
 中東地緣衝突短期難解，油價維持高位但震盪加劇。
 短期美國雙率走高 ，使金價短期承壓 ，惟美歐日財政赤字壓力與去美元化 ，使全球央行與長期投資
人買盤依舊，提供黃金中長期穩定支撐。

### Page 18

個別客戶因年齡、投資能力、金融商品交易經驗及風險承受度不同，適合之服務及/或金融商品亦有不同，應進一步與專屬理財業務人員進行評估。投資人應徹底瞭解相關服務及金融商品之風險 ，並審慎評估個人財力、資產狀況、
理財需求及風險承受能力，獨立判斷並自行決定是否投資及承擔有關風險。投資任何商品皆具有風險，商品以往之績效不保證未來之投資收益，本公司不負責商品之盈虧，也不保證最低之收益。
本公司保留對本文件及其內容之著作權及其他相關權利，任何人未經本公司同意，不得翻印或作其他任何用途使用(包括但不限於傳送或轉發予其他人士等)。如有違反情事，本公司除不負任何責任外，亦將對未經同意之使用與散布
等行為究責。
基金警語
投資一定有風險，基金投資有賺有賠，申購前應詳閱公開說明書
【信託產品投資須知及風險預告：（請投資人審慎評估下列各項條文內容）】
1. 基金（包括具有定期配息之基金）投資具投資風險，此一風險可能使信託本金發生全部虧損。
2. 基金委託人須自負盈虧，本公司受託投資不保本不保息，除盡善良管理人注意義務外，投資所可能產生的本金虧損、匯率損失、或基金解散、清算、移轉、合併等風險，均由委託人承擔。

3. 本公司受託投資之基金經金管會核准或同意生效，惟不表示絕無風險。基金經理公司以往之經理績效不保證基金之最低投資收益；基金經理公司除盡善良管理人之注意義務外，不負責基金之盈虧，亦不保證最低之收益，委託人
申購前應詳閱基金公開說明書。
4. 投資前應詳閱風險預告及各該基金公開說明書 ，確實作好財務規劃與風險評估 。本公司受託投資之基金已備有公開說明書 (或其中譯本)或投資人須知【前述文件併揭露有關基金應負擔之費用 (境外基金含分銷費用)】，委託人可
至本公司營業處所索取或至公開資訊觀測站、基金資訊觀測站或本公司網站下載。
5. 為保護既有投資人之權益，部份境外基金設有價格稀釋調整機制及公平市價規定，投資人申購前應詳閱基金公開說明書及投資人須知。
6. 委託人瞭解並同意受託人辦理特定金錢信託業務(即委託人委託投資基金)交易時，自交易相對人取得之報酬、費用、折讓等各項利益，得作為受託人收取之信託報酬；另委託人在持有基金期間 ，受託人仍持續收受經理費分成報
酬，委託人可至受託人(即本公司)網站 (https://www.kgi.com.tw/zh-tw/)查詢基金之通路報酬。其基金經理費率及其分成費率與其他通路報酬最新變動情形，亦可至該網站查詢。

7. 後收型基金在贖回時，基金公司將依持有期間長短收取不同比率之遞延申購手續費 ，該費用將自贖回總額中扣除；另後收型基金若有收取分銷費 ，則申購手續費雖可遞延收取，惟每年仍需支付0%~1.06%的分銷費（按基金日平
均淨資產價值計算），可能造成實際負擔費用增加（分銷費依各基金公司而有不同，請參考本公司後收型基金投資費用暨特約事項及各基金之投資人須知），另該費用已反映於每日基金淨值中，委託人無需額外支付。
8. 委託人之交易如經基金公司認定為短線交易者，基金公司或本公司可拒絕受理申請轉換或申購要求，並可請求支付一定比例之贖回費用或其他相關費用，費用標準均依各該基金公司之規定。
9. 委託人因不同時間進場，將有不同之投資績效，過去之績效亦不代表未來績效之保證。基金配息率不代表基金報酬率，且過去配息率不代表未來配息率；基金淨值可能因市場因素而上下波動，投資人於獲配息時，宜一併注意基
金淨值之變動。又投資新興市場可能比投資已開發國家有較大的價格波動及流動性較低的風險，委託人應慎選投資標的。
10. 部分基金可能持有衍生性商品部位，該部位可能超過基金淨資產價值之100%，並可能因而造成基金淨值高度波動及衍生其他風險。

11. 基金的配息可能由基金的收益或本金中支付。任何涉及由本金支出的部分，可能導致原始投資金額減損；且部分基金配息前未先扣除應負擔之費用。由本金支付配息之相關資料，投資人可至證券投資信託事業或總代理人之公司
網站查詢。
12. 非投資等級債券基金（本類基金主要係投資於非投資等級之高風險債券且配息來源可能為本金）（主要特色係投資前揭基金之組合基金亦同），適合能承受較高風險之『非保守型』委託人且不宜占投資組合過高的比重。由於非
投資等級債券之信用評等未達投資等級或未經信用評等，且對利率變動的敏感度甚高，可能會因利率上升、市場流動性下降，或債券發行機構違約不支付本金、利息或破產而蒙受虧損，不適合無法承擔相關風險之委託人；又如
係本國投信機構募集者，則可能投資於符合美國Rule 144A規定具有私募性質之債券，雖其投資總金額不得超過基金淨資產價值之百分之三十，然該債券具有債券發行人違約之信用風險、利率風險、流動性風險、交易對手風險
及因財務訊息揭露不完整或因價格不透明導致波動性較大之風險，投資人應審慎評估；另證券投資信託事業募集之債券型基金、平衡型基金、不動產證券化基金、以追蹤、模擬或複製標的指數表現之指數股票型基金（ETF）及

指數型基金或募集之基金投資新興市場國家債券達基金淨資產價值之百分之六十以上者，或有可能投資符合美國Rule 144A規定具有私募性質之債券，亦應留意相關風險。
13. 投資於中國之基金應遵守中華民國相關法令之規定：境外基金 (不含經金管會核准向證交所申請上市或交易之境外 ETF)如有投資大陸地區之有價證券者，以掛牌上市有價證券及銀行間債券市場為限 ，且投資前述有價證券總金額
不得超過該境外基金淨資產價值之百分之二十(境外基金機構依鼓勵境外基金深耕計畫向金管會申請並經認可者得適用鼓勵境外基金深耕計畫所列放寬前揭投資總金額上限至百分之四十之優惠措施 )；證券投資信託基金雖可投資
大陸地區之有價證券，惟仍應符合信託契約、公開說明書、證券投資信託基金管理辦法等相關規定及受到基金公司QFII額度之限制。又投資人須留意中國市場之政府政策、法令、會計稅務制度、經濟與市場等變動所可能產生之
投資風險。
14. 各基金於公開（含簡式）說明書或投資人須知揭露之風險報酬等級，係依據投信投顧公會「基金風險報酬等級分類標準」而訂定，本公司亦依據投信投顧公會「基金風險報酬等級分類標準」訂定基金產品風險等級。

富管理客服專線或透過財富管理客服信箱服務，查詢申訴案件處理進度。

2
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
BULLETIN
The monthly household spending indicator rose 0.6%mth in
September (nominal), exceeding both Westpac and market
expectations. We had anticipated a 0.5%mth contraction
(on the basis of a softer read on vehicle sales in the month),

while the market had pencilled in a 0.2%mth rise. Today’s
result instead marks a return to growth following August’s
brief contraction (revised down 0.1ppt to –0.1%mth), with
annual growth strengthening to 6.8%yr. For Q3 as a whole,
nominal spending rose 2.1%qtr, the strongest quarterly
result since the December quarter of 2022. In real terms,
volumes increased a more subdued 0.7%qtr, pointing to a
sharp acceleration in price growth through the quarter.

Non-discretionary spending outperforms
By broad category, services spending more than reversed
last month’s decline, rising a solid 1.1%mth (+6.8%yr). Goods
spending held around its recent pace, increasing 0.3%mth
(+6.7%yr). Discretionary spending returned to growth in
September, rising 0.3%mth (+6.9%yr), although this failed to
fully unwind August’s decline.
The relative strength in non-discretionary spending observed
last month continued into September, with the category rising

1.2%mth (+6.5%yr). While the headline result was stronger,
the breakdown aligns with findings from our Westpac–DataX
Card Tracker, and Westpac-DataX Consumer Panel  which
show discretionary goods continuing to underperform other
categories (see here and here).
At the detailed level, transport and health led the gains,
both rising 1.5%mth. The increase in transport spending was
largely attributable to higher fuel spending, up 5.4%mth,
reflecting elevated fuel prices, with volumes down 2.2%mth.

The ABS also noted higher spending on vehicle repairs
and maintenance, while domestic air travel increased as
households travelled to attend major sporting events.
Recreation & culture rose 0.8%mth, while miscellaneous
goods & services (+0.6%mth), hotels, cafes & restaurants
(+0.5%mth), and clothing & footwear (+0.1%mth) also
recorded gains.
Partially offsetting these increases, food spending declined
0.1%mth, while furnishings & household equipment fell

0.2%mth, likely reflecting the ongoing weakness in housing
turnover (see here). Alcoholic beverages & tobacco recorded
the largest decline, falling 0.7%mth.
State outcomes were positive across the board in September.
WA led the gains, rising 1.1%mth and reversing last
month’s decline. Victoria also rebounded (+1.0%mth),
while Queensland (+0.6%mth), SA (+0.6%mth), Tasmania
(+0.5%mth) and NSW (+0.3%mth) all recorded increases.

are based are reasonable, the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
3
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
BULLETIN
Volume growth remains firm 
Nominal spending rose a firm 2.1%qtr in Q3, while volumes 
increased a more subdued 0.7%qtr, matching the pace recorded 
in Q2. This implies a sharp acceleration in price growth to 
1.4%qtr, from 0.6%qtr in Q2, and marks the strongest quarterly 
increase in prices since the December quarter of 2022. Through 
the year, volume growth strengthened to 3.2%yr, remaining 
well above the 1.8%yr average recorded through 2025.
Transport led gains in volume terms, rising 1.6%qtr, 
likely partly reflecting base effects in air travel following 
disruptions associated with the Middle East conflict in the June 
quarter. Clothing & footwear (+1.2%qtr), health (+1.1%qtr), 
miscellaneous goods & services (+1.1%qtr), food (+0.5%qtr), 
recreation & culture (+0.5%qtr), and furnishings & household 
equipment (+0.1%qtr) also recorded increases. Hotels, cafes 
& restaurants spending declined 0.1%qtr in volume terms, 
while alcoholic beverages & tobacco was the main drag, falling 
1.2%qtr.
Most states recorded real spending growth over the quarter, 
led by WA (+1.4%qtr), Victoria (+0.9%qtr), NSW (+0.5%qtr), 
Queensland (+0.5%qtr) and SA (+0.4%qtr). Tasmania lagged its 
state peers, recording a 0.1%qtr decline.
Conclusion
The household spending indicator provides a useful guide to 
around two-thirds of total consumer expenditure. Importantly, 
it excludes several major categories included in the National 
Accounts measure of real household consumption, including 
rents and utilities.

Today’s volumes outcome was broadly in line with our 
expectations, with Westpac anticipating around a 0.6%qtr rise 
in Q3, and remains consistent with our view on consumption 
ahead of the Q3 National Accounts (due 2 December). 
However, this will likely be the high water mark for spending. 
The renewed deterioration in global energy markets and the 
cumulative effect of four rate increases over the first three 
quarters of the year are likely to weigh more heaveily on 
household spending in Q4.

Looking further ahead, households will continue to face 
a restrictive monetary policy environment, with the RBA 
delivering an additional rate increase at yesterday’s November 
meeting (see here). Combined with persistent consumer 
pessimism, ongoing cost-of-living pressures (see here) and a 
housing downturn impacting household wealth, spending looks 
set to slow further in 2027.

RBNZ の予想外の利上げ見送りで、NZ ドル相場は一段と弱含むか 
～追加利上げの時期や総選挙を巡る不透明感が重なり、豪ドルとの対照的な動きが続くか～ 
 経済調査部 主席エコノミスト  西濵 徹 
（℡：050-5474-7495）

（要旨） 
○ ニュージーランド準備銀行（RBNZ）は、10 月 27～28 日に開催した定例理事会において、政
策金利を2.75％に据え置くことを決定した。RBNZ は７月に約３年ぶりの利上げに舵を切り、
９月にも追加利上げを実施するなど金融引き締めを強めてきた。このため、今回の据え置き
は３会合ぶりであり、金融市場の事前予想に反する決定となった。 
○ ７-９月のインフレ率は前年比＋3.7％と前期から鈍化したものの、コアインフレ率は加速す
るなど、インフレ圧力は依然として根強いことが確認された。このため、金融市場において
は RBNZ が今回利上げに動くことを織り込む動きが広がりをみせていた。こうしたなか、 今回
の決定は４対３の僅差となったほか、声明文や総裁会見でも追加利上げの必要性が示された
ものの、そのタイミングについては不透明感が残る。 
○ また、11 月７日の総選挙を控え、RBNZ の政策目標の見直しが争点となっていることも、 先行
きの政策運営を巡る不透明感につながっている。金融市場では、予想外の金利据え置きに対
する 「失望」 に加え、 金融政策が後手に回るとの警戒感もＮＺドル相場の重しとなっている。
当面はＮＺドル相場の上値が重い展開が続くと見込まれ、オセアニア通貨のなかでも豪ドル
とＮＺドルが対照的な動きをみせる可能性がある。

【RBNZは事前予想に反して、10 月の定例会合で３会合ぶりに政策金利を据え置く決定】 
ニュージーランド準備銀行（RBNZ）は、10 月 27～28 日に開催した定例理事会において、政策金
利であるオフィシャル・キャッシュ・レート（ＯＣＲ）を2.75％に据え置くことを決定した。ニュ
ージーランドでは、2025 年以降にインフレ率が加速し、RBNZ の定める目標レンジ（１～３％）の
上限を上回る伸びで推移している。 さらに、 イラン情勢の悪化を受けた原油価格の上昇がエネルギ
ーインフレを招いたことから、RBNZ は７月の定例会合で約３年ぶりの利上げに舵を切り、 ９月会合
でも追加利上げに動くなど、金融引き締めを強化してきた（注１）。このため、RBNZ による金利据

  （https://www.dlri.co.jp/report/macro/654752.html）

え置きは３会合ぶりとなる。 
RBNZ のブレマン総裁は、 ９月の定例会合後の記者会見において、 追加利上げに含みを持たせる考
えを示していた。一方で、予め決められた方針に固執せず、追加利上げのタイミングは極めて不確
実と述べるとともに、これまでの利上げの効果などを見極める考えをみせた。声明文でも、２会合
連続の利上げ実施を受けて、 先行きは大幅利上げに動く必要性は低下したとの認識を示すなど、「タ
カ派」姿勢が後退している様子がうかがえた。こうした姿勢の変化もあり、ＲＢＮＺによる断続的
な利上げ実施にもかかわらず、その後のＮＺドル相場は調整の動きを強めた。 
同国では、11 月７日に議会（代議院：一院制）の総選挙が実施されるが、RBNZ のマンデート（政
策目標）が争点のひとつとなっていることも、ＮＺドル相場が調整の動きを強める一因となった。
最大野党の左派・労働党を中心とする政
党連合は、マンデートに持続可能な雇用
の最大化を再び加えることを公約に掲げ
ている。世論調査では、右派の国民党を
中心とする与党連合、左派連合の競り合
いが続いており、新興政党・オポチュニ
ティ党の動向が注目されている（注２）。
足元の雇用環境をみると、 直近４-６月の
失業率が 10 年ぶりの高水準となってお
り（図１）、 仮にマンデートが変更されれ
ば、利上げのハードルが高まる可能性が
ある。こうした事情もＮＺドル相場の重しになったと考えられる。 
一方で、RBNZ のブレマン総裁は、９月の定例会合後の記者会見において、選挙結果が政策運営に
影響を与えることはないとの見方を示し
た。このため、金融市場では今回の定例
会合を占ううえで、物価動向に注目した。
７-９月のインフレ率は前年比＋3.7％と
５四半期連続で目標レンジを上回ったも
のの、 前期 （同＋4.1％） から鈍化し、RBNZ
が９月の定例会合で示した見通し（前年
比＋3.9％）も下回った（図２） 。コアイ
ンフレ率は前年比＋ 2.9％と目標レンジ

2.6％） から加速し、７四半期ぶりの高い

伸びとなるなど、 インフレ圧力が高まっている様子が確認された。このため、 金融市場ではRBNZ が
追加利上げに動くとの見方が広がったものの、予想に反して政策金利を据え置いた格好となった。

【僅差での据え置き決定、市場の「失望」を反映してNZドル相場は上値の重い展開が続くか】 
会合後に公表された声明文では、今回の決定について「４（据え置き）対３（25bp の利上げ）の
賛成多数で金利据え置きを決定した」とした。足元の物価動向について「想定を下回った」 、 「賃金
上昇が抑制されており、 中期的なインフレ期待は安定している」 とする一方、 「コアインフレ率は目
標レンジの上限近くに加速しており、物価上昇の動きが広範に 及びつつある」との見方を示した。
先行きについては「世界的な情勢が短期的にインフレ圧力を一段と強めている」として、その要因
に「原油高によるエネルギー価格の上昇と通貨ＮＺドル安」 を挙げた。 そして、 「10-12 月のインフ
レ率は＋4.4％に加速が見込まれる」としつつ、 「原油や石油製品価格の変動が激しく、インフレ見
通しは不透明」との見方を示した。 
実体経済については「回復が続いているものの、状況には依然ばらつきがある」との見方を示し
た。具体的には「外需関連産業では、商品市況の上昇、世界的な需要の堅調さ、外国人観光客数の
増加の恩恵を受けている」一方、 「内需関連産業は低調に推移している」とした。一方で、 「オーク
ランドを中心に住宅投資の回復の兆しがでている」とした。 
政策運営については「物価に対する一時的な要因を差し引いた上で、 経済に不必要な不安定を起
こすことを回避しつつ、中期的にインフレ率を目標レンジの中央（２％）に戻すことに注力してい
る」とあらためて強調した。 そのうえで、 「家計消費が弱く、 一部の企業ではコスト上昇の転嫁が制
限されている」 としつつ、 「インフレ率が高止まりすれば、 コスト上昇がインフレ期待や価格や賃金
に波及してインフレがより持続的なものとなるリスクが高まる」との見方を示した。 
世界経済については「堅調に推移しているものの、地政学リスクやエネルギーインフレ、金融市
場のボラティリティの高まり、ＡＩ（人工知能）関連投資の急増などを背景に、その脆弱性は高ま
っている」との見方を示した。一方で、同国経済について「回復の動きが予想以上に力強くなる可
能性はある一方、世界経済の悪化、外需の好調さが雇用や所得、家計消費に波及しなければ勢いを
失う可能性がある」との認識を示した。そのうえで、今回の決定について「政策委員は全体的なイ
ンフレ見通しでは一致したが、個々のリスクに対する見方や金融政策の対応時期に関する意見は割
れた」、先行きについても「追加利上げが必要となる可能性は高いとの見方で一致したものの、 その
タイミングは経済見通し次第」として、追加利上げに含みを持たせる考えを示した。 
会合後に記者会見に臨んだブレマン総裁は、今回の協議について「景気動向を勘案すれば、より
抑制的な政策が必要になることで合意した」 、 「ＮＺドル安が続けば輸入インフレを助長する可能性
がある」との認識が共有されたとした。先行きについては「さらなる引き締めが必要となり、状況
によっては引き締め的な領域に入る可能性がある」 、 「今後の会合で追加利上げを行う可能性が高い」
との認識を示した。そして、 「ＯＣＲの道筋が９月会合の想定を上回る可能性についても協議した」
としつつ、 「中立金利の見通しには幅があり、 常に不確実性がある」 として、 具体的な水準への言及
は避けた。一方で、 今回の決定については「経済見通しに基づいた決定である」 としたうえで、 「イ

なく変更されることがあります。また、記載された内容は、第一生命保険ないしはその関連会社の投資方針と常に整合的であるとは限
りません。

ンフレ率を目標レンジに戻すための条件は依然として良好」 、 「賃金上昇率は目標とするインフレ動
向に見合う水準を下回っている」との認識を示した。 
前述したように、金融市場においては今回の利上げを織り込む動きが広がっていたため、予想外
の金利据え置き決定に対しては『失望』
にも似た反応がみられ、 ＮＺドル相場は

ることがある。一部では、金融政策が後

と考えられる。このため、当面のＮＺド

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
3
OVERVIEW
Coming into the home strait for 2026,
the Australian consumer has again
got the staggers. Spending has held
up surprisingly well given the intense
headwinds that have buffeted household
budgets over the year. But the same cannot
be said for sentiment which has fallen off
badly in the face of more shocks.

Our October Red Book surveys the scene.
Psychologically, consumers has seen a
return to the ‘cost of living nightmare’
they struggled with through much of
2022, 2023 and 2024. Global forces
and domestic factors are again having
pervasive negative impacts on budgets.
The scale may not be as severe as the
previous episode – inflation is running in
the 3-4% range not the 7-8% and interest
rates have risen 1ppt not 4 – but the
feeling is similar. In part this because there

has was very little reprieve in between –
despite tax cuts and a partial unwind of the
interest rate tightening in 2025, household
incomes never got a chance to regain the
lost ground.
The inflation ‘relapse’, and the difficulty
the RBA and governments seem to be
having in managing the situation, is likely
feeding into fears about the outlook for the
wider economy as well.
Exactly what this means for spending is
not entirely clear. As discussed in our last

report , sentiment reads have been much
weaker than actual spending in recent
years. The apparent disconnect is likely
due to a mix of legacy effects from COVID,
and the specific challenges posed by high
inflation (including the lingering effects
of high prices even once inflation has
subsided).
On balance we expect the spending side
to slow materially, particularly as we
head into year end. Indeed, the seven
weeks between now and Christmas
could prove decisive for the consumer

outlook, beginning with another important
sentiment survey update and then the high
season for retail. How spending performs
outside of key sales windows will be most
telling.
Staggering into year end

1. Consumer spending: slowdown ahead
“... sentiment ... has fallen off badly in the face of
more shocks”

### Page 4

or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
4
The Westpac–Melbourne Institute
Consumer Sentiment Index declined 4.2%
over the three months to Oct, unwinding a
mini-rally through the middle of the year.
At 80.4 the average read over the Oct
survey implies that pessimists outnumber
optimists by nearly 20%, a deeply
pessimistic level by historical standards

that is amongst the forty worst reads since
the survey began in the 1973.
The survey detail shows concerns are still
primarily about the cost-of-living and high
interest rates than the more acute job and
insolvency problems that arise during a
recession. The RBA’s Sep rate hike, mid-
way through the survey week looks to have
badly rattled consumers.
The component detail shows a broad
pull-back but with bigger declines in
assessments of family finances and

buyer sentiment and milder declines in
assessments of the economic outlook.
Consumers remain intensely risk averse.
The Westpac Risk Aversion Index held near
historical highs in Sep, readings still only a
touch of the 52-year high of 60.8 recorded
in June 2023. Recall that the index is based
on responses to questions on the ‘wisest
place for savings’. Responses show ‘safe
options’ remain very heavily favoured.
The sentiment mix points to a significant

weakening in spending momentum is
weakening. CSI±, a modified indicator
that shows a better correlation with per
capita spending, points to a shift back to
significant outright contractions in per
capita spending. That said, spending
in 2026 has held up better than these
indicators have suggested so far.
Consumers are on high alert for interest
rate. The Westpac–Melbourne Institute
Mortgage Rate Expectations Index rose
5.5% to 179 over the three months to

Oct, just shy of May’s cycle peak of 181.
The RBA has raised interest rates 25bps
since then. The Nov survey update due Nov
10 will be an important update on how
expectations have evolved.
The ‘time to buy a major item’ index
dropped 4.3% over the last three months,
taking it back near the lows seen earlier
in the year and a full 40pts below the
long run avg. Durables spend has been
mixed over the first half of 2026, posting a
surprisingly solid gain in Q2 but with most

of this centred on new vehicle spend where
‘extenuating circumstances’ were at play.
Homebuyer sentiment has been
understandably unsettled in recent months
given heightened uncertainty around
interest rate and tax policy changes amid
evidence of a broadening downturn in
established markets.
The ‘time to buy a dwelling’ index actually
lifted 3.6% over the three months to Oct
but is still at firmly pessimistic levels. Both
monthly reads and sub-group detail have

been very unsettled.
The broadening and deepening downturn
in Australia’s housing markets is seeing
an equally rapid shift in consumer house
price expectations although these have not
turned as negative as they did during the
2022-23 correction.
The Westpac–Melbourne Institute
Consumer House Price Expectations Index
declined a further 2.4% over the 3mths to
Oct, following a steep 25% drop over the
previous 3mths. At 115.6, the Oct index
read is miles below the 15yr high of 174 at

the start of the year and below the long run
avg of 130. That said, the read is still above
100, meaning more consumers expect
prices to rise than fall.
The labour market remains an island of
relative calm for consumers. The Westpac–
Melbourne Institute Unemployment
Expectations Index rose 9.3% over the
three months to Oct, reversing most of
the 12.1% improvement over the previous
three months (recall that higher reads mean
more consumers expect unemployment to

rise over the year ahead). At 142.2, the Oct
index read is now decisively above the long
run avg of 129 again but  still well below
the levels that would sound more worrying
alarms.
As noted, the Nov survey, currently in the
field and due to be released on Nov 10,
will provide critical updates on many of
these themes.
Quick run-down

### Page 5

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
5
THE CONSUMER MOOD
• Consumers remain stuck in a cost-of-
living nightmare that seems to have
not end in sight. Sentiment remains
deeply pessimistic, weighed down by
continued uncertainty about the path
for inflation and interest rates, the
latter providing a clear negative ‘jolt’
midway through the Oct survey week.

• The Westpac Melbourne Institute
Index of Consumer Sentiment declined
4.2% over the three months to Oct,
unwinding a mini-rally through the
middle of the year. At 80.4 the average
read over the Oct survey implies that
pessimists outnumber optimists by
nearly 20%, a deeply pessimistic level
by historical standards. It is amongst
the forty worst reads since the survey
began in the 1973. We have had
two others in this unfortunate group
already this year (in Apr and Jun) with

another eight in 2022–23. Altogether
this marks the worst period of recurring
extremely weak sentiment since the
disastrous recession in the early 1990s.
• The survey detail shows consumer
concerns are still primarily about the
cost-of-living and high interest rates
than the more acute job and insolvency
problems that arise during a recession.
• Average weekly pump prices pushed
back over $2.30/litre nationally in early
Oct, rising to over $2.35/litre since. This

is near the peaks seen back in April and
up nearly 25% since the start of the
year. Meanwhile the RBA interest rate
rise in late Sep took the cash rate to
4.6%, the highest since 2011, with the
standard variable mortgage interest
rate pushing above 9% for the first time
since 2008.
• The RBA’s Sep move badly rattled
consumers. Sentiment amongst the
60% of the sample surveyed prior came
in at 86.9, up slightly from the Sep
read. The sentiment read across the

40% surveyed after the RBA decision
came in at just 67.2, an alarmingly weak
read that, for complete surveys, has
only been registered during the depths
of the early 1990s recession. The nearly
20% drop between pre and post RBA
samples is also the largest negative
swing we have seen since we started
tracking daily responses in 2019.
• The RBA has delivered another hike as
we go to press, meaning Nov is likely
to see yet another interest rate hike

to sentiment (the survey result will be
released on Nov 10).

3. Consumer sentiment: finances, economic conditions

### Page 6

or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
6
THE CONSUMER MOOD
• The component detail shows a broad
pull-back but with bigger declines in
assessments of family finances and
buyer sentiment. The ‘finances vs a
year ago’ sub-index dropped 6% over
the 3mths to Oct  to just 66.9, on a par
with April’s extreme low. The pressure
is not expected to ease up any time

soon, the ‘finances, next 12 months’
sub-index also down 8.3% to 88.4, in
the bottom 10% of reads for this sub-
index since the survey began.
• Consumer views on the economy
recorded milder declines, the ‘economy,
next 12 months’ sub-index unchanged
on Jul, holding at 78.3 and the
‘economy, next 5 years’ sub-index down
1.8% to 85.5. However, the latter is now
back near the lows seen in Aug 2020.
This was a dark time during the COVID
crisis when Melbourne had entered

its second lockdown and just prior to
the Federal government significantly
expanding its JobKeeper supports. The
readings suggest there are shades of
COVID now with a similar sense of
helplessness – that Australia is unable
to avoid a global inflation problem and
that there is relatively little authorities
can do to help the situation that does
not add to pressures on consumers.
• The cost of living and interest rate
pressures are both broad and intense.

Pessimists outnumber optimists in 102
of the 106 sub-groups we track, and by
over 20% in nearly two thirds of these.
This speaks to the pervasive influence
of higher fuel costs in particular.
• That said, sentiment is becoming more
dispersed. It is possible to compute
sentiment index scores for each
individual respondent. Chart 4 shows
how the spread across these scores
has evolved since 1995. The average
standard deviation over the full history
is 46pts. It was about 4pts lower over

the decade prior to COVID but has risen
steadily since to over 52pts in Oct, the
average spread over the last 12mths
marking a record high. Chart 5 shows
the full distribution of individual scores
in the last 3mths compared to history.
• We noted in our last report that the
widening spread has been accompanied
by a notable divergence in sentiment
across major and minor party voters.
There are likely multiple underlying
reasons for the shift which the voter

group split may be picking up indirectly
rather than directly due to the way these
skew across different demographics.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
7
Spending: squeeze ahead
• Our CSI± composite combines sub-
indexes tracking views on ‘family
finances’ and ‘time to buy a major
item’ with the Westpac Consumer Risk
Aversion Index and usually provides a
good guide to trends in spending over
the next 3–6mths.
• However, as discussed in detail in our
last report the composite has been of

more limited use in recent years due
to both the volatile spending around
COVID lock-downs and the nature of
the post-COVID cost-of-living shock
to sentiment. Analysis shows adding
the WestpacMelbourne Institute Index
of Unemployment Expectations (p15)
to the composite goes some way to
redressing these issues. Accordingly,
our CSI± indicator will now include this
additional component going forward.
As Chart 6 highlights, the composite
should still mainly be viewed as a

directional guide to where per capita
spending growth momentum is heading
rather than a point estimate.
•  Unsurprisingly, the CSI± indicator fell
steeply over the 3mths to Oct, dropping
from 75.6 to 67.6, putting the measure
over 26pts below its long run avg of
93.8.
• The move reflected the more marked
falls in assessments of finances
and ‘time to buy’ with a significant
deterioration in unemployment
expectations as well. Risk aversion
remained unchanged at very high levels.

• Chart 6 shows the current read is
consistent with a large contraction in
real per capita consumer spending,
even allowing for the wedge that has
opened up between observed spending
growth and the CSI± indicator over the
last few years.
• The Q2 national accounts showed some
slowing in consumer spending although
it was more resilient than feared to the
combined effect of higher fuel prices
and interest rates while incomes posted
a robust rise. More generally a range

of shocks and shifts made for a messy
quarter (see here for more).
• Real consumption rose 0.4%qtr taking
annual growth to 1.8%yr, down from
a peak of 2.7%yr in Q3 last year. The
detail showed declines in fuel spend
and transport services (including air
travel) were more than offset by a 10%
surge in vehicle sales, led by a big rise
in EVs and hybrids.

7. Annual national accounts: benchmark revisions
SENTIMENT INDICATORS

### Page 8

or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
8
• Household disposable incomes rose
0.6%qtr, lifting annual growth to
2.3%yr, supported by a continued
robust rise in labour income (more due
to average compensation than head
count in the latest quarter). This in
turn meant the household savings rate
ticked up slightly from 6.4% to 6.5%.
• New annual benchmarks released by

the ABS since the Q2 accounts were
published showed relatively minor
revisions (Chart 7). For the full financial
year, annual growth in disposable
income was nudged 0.2pts lower to
2.1%yr with real spending shaved back
from 1.8%yr to 1.7%yr and the savings
rate lowered from 6.5% to 6.1%. These
changes will be rolled into the quarterly
estimates when the Q3 national
accounts are released on Dec 2.
• Partial data suggests Q3 has seen a lift
in nominal spend although most of this

is a price effect. The ABS household
spending indicator, which covers around
two thirds of the total consumption
basket, showed a 2.1%qtr rise in nominal
terms in Q3 up from 1.3%qtr in Q2.
However, all of that was due to stronger
price growth with volumes posting a
0.7% rise in line with Q2 (see here).
• Our Westpac-DataX Card Tracker Index
foreshadowed the spending indicator
result and suggests quarterly growth
momentum is still tracking at around 2%

in mid-Oct in nominal terms. That said,
quarterly growth rate is likely slowing in
real terms given our estimates that Oct
saw a further step-up in price growth.
The latest monthly pace of gains also
looks to be about flat in nominal terms
(see Chart 8).
• Private sector business surveys point
to weak conditions for retail (consistent
with under-performing discretionary
goods segments) and stalling activity
for consumer services. Near term
expectations are at similar levels for

both but with year-weak expectations
flat rather than negative for retail.
• The ABS will publish full spending
estimates with the Q3 national accounts
on Dec 2. Based on partials we expect
these to show a solid +0.8%qtr gain,
lifting annual growth to 2.1%yr, an
upgrade on the +0.4%qtr, 1.5%yr
forecast in or last report. However, that’s
expected to be the high water mark for
growth with forecast to slow abruptly
into year end and early 2027, growth

tracking back to 1.4%yr by Q3 2027.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
9
Interest rates: hike fears realised
SENTIMENT INDICATORS
• Consumers have been on high alert for
interest rate rises for some time. Those
fears materialised in late Sep and again
in early Nov. The Oct survey was in the
field during the week of the Sep move.
Not surprisingly, the Westpac–Melbourne
Institute Mortgage Rate Expectations

Index, which tracks consumer interest
rate expectations, rose 5.5% to 179, just
shy of May’s cycle peak of 181.
• Amongst those surveyed after the RBA
decision, just over 80%, an overwhelming
majority, expect mortgage rates to
increase further over the next 12mths.
This compares to 64% in Sep. Across
the mortgage belt, the share is closer to
90%, with over 40% of this sub-group
expecting rates to rise by more than a
percentage point over the next twelve

months.
• While the Sep and Nov interest rate
increases were widely expected, the
scale of the rise over the last 12mths,
a full 1.25%, has come as a surprise.
This time last year, consumers were in
two minds, 39% of those with a view
expecting interest rates to rise and 37%
expecting them to fall. Only 20% of
consumers expected mortgage rates to
rise by over 1ppt.
• As at Oct, just over 40% of consumers
with a view expected mortgage interest
rates to rise by more than 1ppt. While

some hawkishness is clearly warranted,
this is extreme – it would take the
standard variable mortgage rate well
above 10%, to levels not seen since the
mid-1990s, thirty years ago.
• Overall, Oct responses suggest
consumers were broadly expecting
another three 25bp rate hikes from
the RBA. The Nov survey, currently in
the field, will capture how expectations
have shifted after the RBA’s more recent
move. While this may have amplified
rate rise fears, the split vote and some

indications from the Governor that there
may be a pause to assess things in Dec
could work the other way (see here for
more).
• The near term inflation outlook
clearly remains challenging. However,
Westpac expects the RBA to leave
rates on hold through the remainder
of 2026. And while there are risks
of a further tightening in early 2027
we think rates will more likely be
maintained at current levels with an
eventual gradual relaxation in policy
beginning in the second half of 2027.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
10
Inflation: still too hot but wages flat
SENTIMENT INDICATORS
•  Inflation has kicked up again over
the last 3mths, led by a resurgence
in global energy prices. Near term
consumer inflation expectations have
risen sharply again as well although
consumer wage expectations have not
moved, if anything moving marginally

lower in the latest month.
• The Sep CPI report showed headline up
1.3%qtr, the annual pace holding at the
Jun qtr pace of 3.9%yr. The key ‘trimmed
mean’ measure lifted 1.0%qtr, the
annual pace ticking up from 3.6%yr to
3.7%yr. The detail revealed underlying
inflationary pressures continuing even
while a moderation in housing inflation
(dwellings in particular) is unfolding
slightly faster than anticipated. Near-
term risks for inflation are to the upside

but more balanced further out (see here)
• Consumer inflation expectations
have moved higher again. After
peaking at 5.9% in Apr, the Melbourne
Institute’s ‘symmetric mean’ measure
of consumers’ year-ahead inflation
expectations eased back to 4.7% in Jul,
ticking up slightly to 4.9% in Aug and
Sep but jumped again back to 5.3%
in Jul. Expectations are likely being
skewed by the jump in fuel prices.
• Scaling according to historical inflation
outcomes suggests current expectations

are consistent with inflation of around
4%yr. It is difficult to look through the
effects of fuel price volatility, which
amplified expectations in Mar and Apr
but dampened them again when the
temporary halving in fuel excise tax
came into effect through Apr-May.
• From the RBA’s perspective, it will be
important for inflation expectations
to track back towards historical levels
in a timely manner as a sign that
expectations more generally remain

firmly anchored at low inflation rates.
• Certainly there are no signs that the
latest lift in inflation is becoming
embedded into expectations for wages.
Consumer assessments of actual wages
lifted a little when the new Award
decision came into effect but have
settled back to 1.4%, a touch above the
Jun qtr avg of 1.2% but below the long
run avg of 1.8%. Expectations have
tracked a slightly softer path, dipping
to 1.2% in Oct, down from a Jun qtr avg

of 1.3% and long run avg of 1.3%. At
face value, expectations are consistent
with a significant deterioration in real
wages.

Durables: soft spend, dire sentiment
SENTIMENT INDICATORS
• Durables spend has been mixed 
over the first half of 2026, posting 
a  surprisingly solid gain in Q2 but 
with most of this centred on new 
vehicle spend where ‘extenuating 
circumstances’ were at play. Even 
excluding this, the flat underlying trend 
is not nearly as weak as the dire reads 
on the ‘time to buy a major item’ sub-
index would suggest. The last 3mths 
has seen the sub-index drop back 
again, a 4.3% decline taking it to 83, 
near the lows seen earlier in the year 
and a full 40pts below the long-run avg 
of 123. 
• As discussed regularly over the last four 
years, the extreme weakness appears 
to relate to the collapse in purchasing 
power since 2022. While prices have 
stabilised somewhat, their still high 
level is casting a dark shadow over 
buyer sentiment. 
• As Chart 18 shows, durable goods prices 
have been more stable since 2024 but 
look to have posted a bigger rise in the 
Sep quarter, our estimates based on 
the CPI detail pointing to annual price 
growth lifting back above 2.1%yr.

• There are a range factors at play here 
for prices. Construction costs have fed 
into the renovation segment of our 
measure. Some major durable items also 
have higher transport costs components. 
However, a notable medium term trend 
has been the absence of trend price 
declines for audio-visual and computing 
equipment, which make up a large part 
of the ‘small durables’ segment. 
• As chart 15 shows, growth in real 
per capita spend on the ‘major item’ 
segments centred on a 10%qtr jump in 
vehicles in Q2, led by strong take-up 
of EVs that was partly in response to 
the sharp move higher in fuel prices 
this year. Excluding vehicles, per capita 
spend was up just 0.3%qtr, 0.8%yr. 
• Partial data suggests Q3 is softer with 
the industry data pointing to a much 
slower 1%qtr gain for vehicle sales 
and the ABS Household Spending 
Indicator detail showing flat volumes for 
furnishings and household equipment. 
Our card data also suggests there 
has been a further shift to outright 
weakness in discretionary goods in the 
Oct month.

Dwellings: pessimistic and uncertain
SENTIMENT INDICATORS
•  Housing-related sentiment has been 
understandably unsettled in recent 
months, reflecting the heightened 
uncertainty around interest rate and 
tax policy changes amid evidence of a 
broadening downturn in established 
markets. 
• Assessments of ‘time to buy’ – 
generally seen as being from the 
perspective of owner occupiers rather 
than investors – have been choppy 
month to month but still in firmly 
pessimistic territory, albeit off the 
extreme lows seen in previous years. 
• The ‘time to buy a dwelling’ index 
actually lifted 3.6% over the 3mths to 
Oct sharply in May (–16%) but tracked 
a volatile monthly path, up 12% at one 
stage but unwinding most of that as 
inflation and rate rise fears returned. 
• At 88.4, the Oct index read is still far 
from positive, over 30pts below the 
long run avg of 119.

• The sub-group detail is also quite 
unsettled. The Oct month improvement 
was more pronounced for outright 
homeowners, retirees, 25–34-year-olds 
and those earning over $80k a year. 
But on a quarterly basis the biggest 
improvement has been amongst those 
aged 50+ and earning over $100k. 
• Interestingly, existing owners of 
residential property investments (25% 
of all respondents) continue to be 
outright positive on ‘time to buy’.
•  By state, buyer sentiment is still a little 
less downbeat in NSW and Vic (both 
state indexes at 93), where dwelling 
prices have seen bigger moves lower, 
but more pessimistic in Qld (85) and 
deeply pessimistic in WA (71) and SA 
(70). 
• The overarching question is whether 
improving affordability – reflecting a 
mix of lower prices and higher interest 
rates – will be sufficient to offset 
unease about the pace of price declines.

13
House prices: uncertain vs downbeat
SENTIMENT INDICATORS
• The broadening and deepening 
downturn in Australia’s housing 
markets is seeing an equally rapid shift 
in consumer house price expectations 
although these have not turned as 
negative as they did during the 2022-23 
correction. 
• The Westpac Melbourne Institute House 
Price Expectations Index declined a 
further 2.4% over the 3mths to Oct, 
following a steep 25% drop over the 
previous 3mths. At 115.6, the Oct index 
read is miles below the 15yr high of 
174 at the start of the year and below 
the long run avg of 130. 
• That said, the read is still above 100, 
meaning more consumers expect prices 
to rise than fall. This compares to the 
low of 91 during the 2022-23 correction 
when the consensus view was for 
continued declines. The Nov survey 
update will be an important gauge 
of whether a negative consensus has 
again emerged about the house price 
outlook. If it does, price momentum is 
likely to remain weak as buyers will be 
more inclined to hold off and sellers 
more willing to accept low prices now.

• The detailed responses in Oct were a 
picture of uncertainty, 46% of those 
surveyed expecting prices to rise, 18% 
expecting no change, 31% expecting 
prices to decline and 5% reporting 
“don’t know”. This was a similar mix to 
Jul.  
• That said, there is little doubt that 
the downturn is deepening. The 
Cotality home value index – covering 
all of Australia – fell 1.2% in Oct, 
annual growth dropping into negative 
(–2.8%yr) for the first time and prices 
down 6.7% from their peak in Mar (see 
here for a full run down). 
• Actual price growth has been broadly 
comparable to consumer house price 
expectations overall (see Chart 18), 
although the latest monthly result 
suggests the latter is set to move 
materially lower. By state, expectations 
have overshot outcomes in NSW and 
Vic, been very closely aligned in Qld but 
undershot to date in WA. 
• Notably, the sub-group detail shows 
price expectations have outright 
pessimistic (sub-100) for older age 
groups.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
14
Risk aversion: bracing
SENTIMENT INDICATORS
•  Consumers remain intensely risk 
averse. The Westpac Consumer Risk 
Aversion Index remained near historical 
extreme highs in Sep, dipping slightly 
from 60.1 to 57.5, but unchanged in 
seasonally adjusted terms (as shown 
on Chart 20). Readings are just a touch 
of the 52-year high of 60.8 recorded in 
June 2023.  
•  Recall that the index is based on 
responses to questions on the ‘wisest 
place for savings’. These ask consumers 
to nominate from a range of options, the 
risk index being the difference between 
the share nominating ‘safe’ options such 
as ‘deposits’ and ‘repay debt’ and the 
share nominating ‘risky’ options such as 
‘shares’ and ‘real estate’. 
• ‘Safe options’ remain very heavily 
favoured: 33% of consumers 
nominating ‘bank deposits’; 24% 
nominating ‘pay down debt’ and 7% 
nominating ‘superannuation’. The share 
nominating ‘real estate’ hold near 
historic lows at just 4.7%.

•  As noted previously, the mix of high 
inflation, interest rate rises, tax policy 
changes, a housing downturn, and 
numerous other factors – including 
conflict in the Middle East, tariffs, 
and a range of disruptions from AI 
– is creating an extremely uncertain 
environment for consumers when it 
comes to financial decisions. 
• This ‘safety first’ mode is typically 
associated with more elevated savings 
rates, increased voluntary debt 
repayments and an aversion to new 
borrowing that drives debt to income 
ratios lower over time. Notably, mix 
of responses on ‘repay debt’ suggests 
the focus is primarily on non mortgage 
rather than mortgage debt.
• The survey detail showed a fair bit 
of variation across sub-groups, with 
material declines in Vic and SA, 35-44 
year olds, retirees and males but rises 
in NSW and amongst 45-54 year olds. 
Note that all of these reads were taken 
from the Sep survey and so pre-date 
the RBA’s rate rise in late Sep.

15
Job security: a wind shift?
SENTIMENT INDICATORS
• For Australian consumers, the labour 
market remains an island of relative 
calm amid the swirling mix of cost-of-
living stresses, rate rise and housing 
downturns. Job-loss fears have moved 
around, most recently rising again over 
the last three months, but the moves 
have been mild compared to wider 
sentiment swings and the overall level 
of concern still relatively mild.  
• The Westpac-Melbourne Institute 
Index of Unemployment Expectations 
rose 9.3% over the three months to 
Oct, reversing most of the 12.1% 
improvement over the previous three 
months (recall that higher reads mean 
more consumers expect unemployment 
to rise over the year ahead). At 142.2, 
the Oct index read is now decisively 
above the long run avg of 129 again but  
still well below the levels that would 
sound more worrying alarms.
• The latest ABS labour force figures 
show employment gains have been 
lumpy month to month but have 
averaged +34k/mth through May-Aug, a 
tick up from the +22k/mth average over 
the six months prior.

• Annual employment growth has 
been slower, holding within the 1.0-
1.5%yr range over the past year or 
so. With the working age population 
expanding 1.7%yr, the mix has seen 
the unemployment rate drift higher, 
reaching 4.7% in Aug vs 4.4% in 
May (see here for more). While there 
is evidence of a little more slack 
emerging in labour markets this 
appears to be mainly coming from a 
combination of increased supply and 
more moderate growth in hiring rather 
than retrenchments. Unfortunately 
changes to the ABS survey mean official 
estimates of both retrenchments and 
expected retrenchment (as shown in 
Chart 26) are unavailable.    
• Based on historical outcomes and 
adjusting for biases, the latest index 
read is roughly consistent with a 0.4-
0.6ppt rise in the unemployment rate, 
which would take it into the 5.2-5.3% 
range by this time next year.
• The sub-group detail shows real 
estate and construction workers led 
the deterioration in Oct, suggesting it 
is linked to interest rate and housing 
market developments.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
16
Queensland
•   The last time we profiled consumer
sentiment in Qld, a year ago, it was
seeing a marked divergence with
the rest of Aus with Qlders much
more pessimistic. Some of that
shift appeared to be linked to the
withdrawal of what were more material
cost-of-living supports provided by
the Qld state government, and an

associated sharper rise in headline
inflation. That in turn suggested
the negative wedge on sentiment
could close up a little. And for a time
this was the case: Qld more-or-less
re-converged sentiment-wise with
the rest of Aus around the start of
2026. However, since then, sentiment
amongst the state’s consumers has
deteriorated more sharply again. At just
76.3 in Oct, the ‘Sunshine state’ is the
gloomiest of the big 3 eastern states
and plumbing 2022-23 lows.

• Component-wise, the 6pt gap with the
rest of Aus in recent months has mainly
centred on differing assessments of the
economic outlook where there has been
a 7-8pt wedge. However, assessments
of finances deteriorated particularly
sharply in the Oct month suggesting the
housing downturn, which came on later
in Qld, may be a factor now as well.
• Qld’ers are also more pessimistic
on time to buy questions (especially
dwellings) but interestingly are less

troubled about unemployment.
• The latter may speak to a different
take on the wider economic and
inflation challenges: as a state, Qld
has tended to see stronger demand
growth (including an expected lift in
infrastructure and Olympics-related
build) that it putting more pressure
on capacity, particularly across its
labour market. Qld’s unemployment
rate is sitting at 4.5% a touch below
the national rate which is unusual
for a state that has tended to see

unemployment track slightly above the
national rate for most of its history (not
because of labour demand issues but
because the strength and composition
of its population growth has tended to
see more ample supply. This excessive
fear factor may be a view in Qld that
Australia’s capacity and inflation
problems may be more substantive and
enduring.
• Looking ahead, housing may be the
next area to weigh more heavily on
sentiment with prices in the state now

showing a very abrupt slowing (p13).

Document 484: 【粤开宏观】10月出口延续高增：AI拉动近四成增长，明年芯片涨价效应或减弱

Source PDF: all report pdf/【粤开宏观】10月出口延续高增：AI拉动近四成增长，明年芯片涨价效应或减弱.pdf

Date: 2026-11-10

### Page 1

证券研究报告 | 宏观深度

宏观研究 
 【粤开宏观】10 月出口延续高增：AI
拉动近四成增长，明年芯片涨价效应
或减弱 
2026 年 11 月 10 日

近期报告  
《 【粤开宏观】 美股大跌： 有哪些原因？对
A 股有何影响？》2026-11-06 
《 【粤开宏观】 美国中期选举如何影响国内
政治、 对华政策及资本市场？》2026-11-04 
《 【粤开宏观】 中国贸易顺差与外部失衡之
辩：六个关键问题》2026-10-27 
《 【粤开宏观】 积极因素持续积累， 资本市
场稳健发展基础不断夯实》2026-10-23 
《 【粤开宏观】 全球K 型分化的三大驱动：
AI、利率与债务》2026-10-20

导读 
11 月 10 日，海关总署公布进出口数据。2026 年 10 月，按美元计价，中国出
口 4123.3 亿美元， 同比增长35.2%， 较上月小幅下降1.1 个百分点； 进口3070.5
亿美元，同比增长 41.6%，较上月加快 10.6 个百分点。出口延续高增长，AI
产业链相关产品贡献突出，其中价格上涨的拉动作用尤为明显；进口增速明
显加快，主要受芯片、能源及部分原材料价格上涨推动。 
1、10 月出口延续高增，AI 产业链支撑突出，汽车及劳动密集型商品出口增
速有所放缓。 分商品看， 一是全球AI 算力需求旺盛， 叠加存储等芯片价格上
涨，推动相关产品出口高增。10 月，集成电路和自动数据处理设备出口同比
分别增长 137.1%和 114.8%， 增速较上月分别提高1.1 和 21.8 个百分点， 合计
拉动整体出口增长 13.6 个百分点，贡献了近四成的出口增幅。其中，集成电
路出口均价同比上涨 143.0%， 出口数量下降2.4%， 价格上涨是出口金额高增
的主要原因。二是汽车出口增速明显放缓，主要受前期对欧盟“抢出口”效
应减弱影响。受中欧混动汽车贸易安排预期影响，部分车企为应对政策调整
的不确定性， 在政策落地前集中出货， 导致10 月汽车出口增速由上月的 62.0%
降至 37.6%。三是劳动密集型商品出口增速有所放缓。10 月，服装、鞋靴、
箱包、玩具和家具出口同比均较上月回落，但仍保持两位数增长。 
分市场看，对韩国、 中国台湾等市场出口较快增长，主要受全球AI 产业链需
求扩张带动；对拉美、非洲出口增速回升，新兴市场基础设施建设和工业化
需求继续提供支撑；对美国、东盟和欧盟出口增速有所回落，但仍保持较快
增长。 
2、10 月进口增速创年内新高，AI 产业链相关产品进口高增与大宗商品涨价
共同推动。2026 年二季度以来，进口增长呈现明显的“价强量弱”特征，价
格上涨对进口金额的拉动较为突出。一是全球 AI 产业链景气延续， 相关产品
进口保持高增长。10 月，集成电路和自动数据处理设备进口金额同比分别增
长 78.3%和 261.0%。其中，集成电路进口均价同比上涨 78.2%，进口数量仅
增长 0.1%，表明全球 AI 算力需求旺盛带动高端芯片价格上涨，是相关产品
进口金额高增的重要原因。二是地缘政治冲突加剧能源供给扰动，国际能源
价格大幅上涨，显著推高进口金额。 10 月，布伦特原油现货均价同比上涨
92.3%， 中国原油、 煤及褐煤、 天然气进口均价分别上涨62.6%、69.4%和 46.6%，
而进口数量分别下降 13.3%、5.5%和增长 2.4%，价格上涨是能源进口金额增
长的主要驱动力。三是部分上游原材料价格上涨， 同样对进口金额形成支撑。
10 月，铜矿砂及其精矿进口金额同比增长23.1%，其中进口均价上涨 39.2%，
进口数量下降 11.6%。 
3、 展望2027 年， 全球AI 需求仍有较强支撑， 但芯片价格涨幅收窄可能推动
中国出口增速放缓。当前， 全球AI 基础设施投资仍处于扩张阶段， 算力建设
及相关设备需求有望延续。尽管美国部分地区数据中心建设监管趋严，可能
影响部分项目的审批进度和区域布局， 但对2027 年既定建设计划的影响相对

### Page 2

宏观深度 
请务必阅读最后特别声明与免责条款 www.ykzq.com  2 / 11 
有限，难以改变全球 AI 投资持续扩张的总体趋势。 
相比需求收缩， 存储芯片价格涨幅收窄对明年中国AI 产业链出口增长的影响
可能更为直接。2026 年相关商品出口高增，既受益于全球 AI 需求扩张，也
受到芯片价格大幅上涨的推动。展望2027 年，随着新增产能逐步释放，存储
价格走势可能分化，叠加今年价格大幅上涨形成的高基数，价格对相关商品
出口金额增长的拉动可能减弱。总体来看， 明年AI 产业链出口仍有较强需求
支撑， 但增速可能较今年放缓， 叠加整体出口高基数及外部贸易摩擦等因素，
预计 2027 年中国整体货物出口同比增长 8%—10%。

风险提示：主要出口市场贸易政策变化超预期、地缘政治冲突演变超预期

一、出口延续高增，AI 产业链仍是核心支撑 
10 月中国出口增速小幅回落，但仍延续强劲增长态势。2026 年 10 月，中国出口同
比增长 35.2%，增速较上月回落 1.1 个百分点，仍创近二十年同期新高。增速边际放缓，
部分源于前期汽车企业在中欧贸易磋商前集中出货，提前释放部分出口需求，随着“抢
出口”效应减弱，相关商品出口增速有所回落。全球制造业景气度维持较高水平，叠加
AI 产业链需求旺盛， 共同支撑中国出口韧性。10 月全球制造业 PMI 为 52.6%， 虽较上月
回落 0.4 个百分点，但已连续15 个月处于扩张区间，表明外需仍较旺盛。同时，AI 产业
链景气延续，相关电子产品出口保持较快增长，中国出口仍具有较强支撑。 
图表1：2026 年 10 月，进出口延续高位增长

资料来源：Wind、粤开证券研究院 
分市场看，10 月中国对主要贸易伙伴出口普遍保持较快增长，其中对韩国、拉美和
非洲出口增速较上月进一步提高。10 月，中国对韩国、拉美和非洲出口分别同比增长
92.4%、32.1%和 19.5%，分别较上月提高 24.3、7.4 和 3.4 个百分点；对东盟、美国、欧
盟出口分别同比增长 46.3%、32.9%和 24.4%， 分别较上月回落 5.9、6.6 和 0.9 个百分点。 
对韩国、中国台湾等市场出口较快增长，主要受益于全球 AI 需求扩张带动的产业
链贸易增长。2026 年 1—9 月，中国对韩国、中国台湾出口的 AI 产业链相关商品分别同
比增长 112.9%和 58.8%，对两地出口增长的贡献率均在八成左右。中国台湾和韩国分别
在先进芯片制造、 计算硬件和存储芯片等环节具有突出优势， 中国则广泛参与电子组件、
设备及配套产品供应。 随着全球AI 需求快速扩张， 产业链上下游跨境协作更加紧密， 带
动中国对韩国、中国台湾相关商品出口快速增长。 
对非洲、拉美等新兴市场出口增速回升，基础设施建设和工业化需求成为 AI 之外
支撑中国装备制造产品出口增长的重要力量。非洲、拉美等地区正处于基础设施完善和
工业化发展的重要阶段，对交通运输、工程建设和工业生产设备的需求持续释放。中国
汽车、叉车、工程机械等产品具有较强的性价比和供应链优势，非洲、拉美已成为相关
商品的重要出口市场，为中国出口增长提供了新的支撑。 
对东盟、美国和欧盟出口增速小幅回落，主要是 9 月高增长后的正常回归，并不意
味着出口需求明显转弱。9 月中国对上述市场出口规模均创年内新高，部分源于国庆假
期前集中出货及中欧贸易磋商前的“抢出口”效应，提前释放了部分出口需求。随着短

期因素消退，10 月出口增速边际回落，但整体仍保持较快增长，出口韧性依然较强。 
图表2：2026 年 10 月，中国对主要经济体出口均维持较快增长

资料来源：海关总署、粤开证券研究院 
分商品看，AI 产业链相关产品出口延续高增，汽车和劳动密集型商品出口增速边际
放缓。 
其一，AI 产业链仍是出口增长的核心驱动力，价格上涨是重要支撑。10 月，集成
电路和自动数据处理设备出口同比分别增长 137.1%和 114.8%，增速较上月分别提高 1.1
和 21.8 个百分点， 合计拉动当月出口增长13.6 个百分点。从量价关系看，出口金额高增
主要由价格上涨驱动，数量表现相对偏弱。其中，集成电路出口均价同比上涨 143.0%，
出口数量下降 2.4%；手机出口金额同比增长 13.2%，出口均价上涨 38.4%，出口数量下
降 18.2%。当前 AI 产业链需求旺盛及相关产品价格上涨持续推升出口金额， 但价格与数
量走势分化，出口增长对价格因素的依赖仍然较高。 
其二，劳动密集型商品出口增速高位回落，但修复趋势延续。10 月，服装、鞋靴、
箱包、玩具和家具出口同比分别增长 19.5%、14.2%、17.9%、27.5%和 13.2%，增速较上
月分别回落 4.3、6.0、4.7、13.3 和 8.8 个百分点，但仍保持两位数增长。1—10 月，上述
五类商品出口累计同比增长 4.5%， 增速较1—9 月提高 1.3 个百分点， 表明劳动密集型商
品出口整体仍处于修复通道。 
其三，汽车出口增速高位回落，主要受前期“抢出口”效应减弱及部分需求提前释
放影响。10 月，汽车（包括底盘）出口金额同比增长37.6%，增速较上月回落 24.4 个百
分点。此前，受中欧混动汽车贸易安排预期影响，部分企业在政策落地前集中出货， 9
月中国对欧盟出口的汽车及其零附件同比增长 82.6%，提前释放了部分后续出口需求。

二、进口增速创年内新高，AI 相关产品与能源涨价共同推动 
10 月进口增速创年内新高，价格上涨是重要推动因素。2026 年 10 月，中国进口同
比增长 41.6%， 增速较上月大幅提高 10.6 个百分点。从量价关系看，2026 年二季度以来，
进口增长呈现明显的“价强量弱”特征。9 月进口价格指数为 128.7，数量指数为 96.8，
分别对应进口价格同比上涨 28.7%、数量同比下降 3.2%，表明此前进口金额高增主要由
价格因素驱动。 
其一，AI 相关商品及上游原材料进口保持高增长，价格上涨贡献突出。10 月，铜
矿砂及其精矿、集成电路和自动数据处理设备进口金额同比分别增长 23.1%、78.3%和
261.0%。其中，铜矿砂及其精矿、集成电路进口均价分别同比上涨 39.2%和 78.2%，涨
幅较上月进一步扩大，而进口数量分别下降 11.6%和增长 0.1%。AI 算力建设需求旺盛，
带动芯片等关键硬件进口增长，相关产品价格上涨进一步推高进口金额。 
其二，能源产品进口增长主要由国际价格上涨驱动。地缘政治冲突加剧能源供给扰
动，推动国际能源价格大幅上涨。10 月布伦特原油现货均价达 125 美元/桶，同比增长
92.3%。10 月， 中国原油、 煤及褐煤、 天然气进口金额同比分别增长41.0%、60.0%和50.2%，
进口均价分别上涨 62.6%、69.4%和 46.6%， 而进口数量分别下降13.3%、5.5%和增长 2.4%。
能源进口金额普遍较快增长， 但实物进口量表现偏弱， 表明进口增长更多来自价格上涨，
而非实际需求扩张。 
图表5：2026 年 2 季度以来，价格因素支撑进口快速增长

资料来源：Wind、粤开证券研究院

三、2027 年中国 AI 出口：美国 AI 需求仍有支撑，价格支撑
可能减弱 
美国中期选举后， 部分地方政府趋严的数据中心建设监管， 引发市场对美国AI 投资
需求及中国相关商品出口增长可持续性的担忧。当前，AI 产业链已成为中国出口增长的
重要引擎， 而美国是全球AI 算力建设的主要需求市场， 地方监管趋严可能增加数据中心
项目落地的不确定性， 进而影响全球AI 硬件需求。 我们认为，美国地方监管趋严可能影
响部分数据中心项目的审批进度和区域布局， 但对2027 年既定建设计划的冲击相对有限，
难以改变 AI 投资持续扩张的总体趋势。 
第一， 美国地方监管收紧对2027 年数据中心建设的直接影响相对有限， 影响可能更
多在 2028 年及以后显现。地方政府的审批限制主要影响尚未开工的新建项目，已获批、
在建项目受影响相对较小。据研究机构 SemiAnalysis 统计，美国前 20 个州计划于 2027
年交付的 33.8 吉瓦数据中心容量中， 约四分之三已经开工， 相关项目建设进度相对确定。
相比之下，计划于2028 年投产的项目仍有较多处于审批、供电工程和施工排期阶段， 更
容易受到地方政策调整的影响。 
第二，美国地方监管收紧难以改变 AI 投资扩张的基本趋势。一方面，地方监管主
要回应居民对电价上涨、电力供应紧张及公共资源占用的担忧，政策重点在于规范数据
中心建设及成本分担，而非限制 AI 产业发展。另一方面，维护 AI 技术领先地位是美国
两党的重要共识，地方监管趋严与联邦政府支持 AI 产业发展的战略方向并不必然冲突。
未来，随着审批标准和成本分担机制逐步明确，部分合规项目有望恢复推进，新增投资
也可能向能源供应充裕、土地成本较低的地区转移。因此，地方监管可能改变数据中心
建设节奏和区域布局，但未必导致全美 AI 算力投资总量明显萎缩。

请务必阅读最后特别声明与免责条款 www.ykzq.com  9 / 11
但仍需警惕 AI 投资预期变化通过供应链“牛鞭效应”放大中国相关商品出口的波
动。全球 AI 硬件产业链分工较长， 美国云厂商的算力投资需求需要经过芯片供应商、 服
务器代工厂及零部件供应商等多个环节，才能最终转化为中国相关商品的出口订单。在
需求扩张阶段，各环节为保障交付、应对供应短缺，往往提前备货、增加安全库存，使
上游订单增长快于终端实际需求； 而当云厂商推迟数据中心建设或下调资本开支预期时，
下游企业可能通过减少备货、推迟采购和消化库存等方式调整订单，导致上游供应商订
单降幅超过终端需求变化。 因此， 即使美国AI 投资需求未出现趋势性下降， 短期预期调
整也可能通过供应链库存周期放大中国 AI 相关商品出口的波动。
而相比需求收缩，存储价格涨幅收窄对 2027 年中国 AI 产业链出口增长的影响可能
更为直接。中国 AI 产业链出口增长主要由存储、 计算等集成电路及搭载相关芯片的自动
数据处理设备带动，2026 年相关商品出口高增中， 价格上涨贡献较大。以集成电路为例，

2026 年 1—10 月，出口均价同比上涨 111.1%，出口数量同比仅增长 3.2%。
2027 年存储价格走势可能分化，叠加 2026 年高基数影响，价格对出口增长的拉动
可能边际减弱。一方面随着新增产能逐步释放、部分终端需求承压，存储产品普遍大幅
涨价的局面可能有所缓解。据研究机构 TrendForce 预测，受 AI 服务器需求增长、HBM
产能挤占及新增产能释放滞后等因素影响，DRAM 供应仍将偏紧；NAND Flash 则呈现
企业级需求旺盛、消费级需求疲弱的分化特征，随着新增产能逐步释放，2027 年下半年
供需可能趋于宽松。另一方面，2026 年存储价格大幅上涨抬高了 2027 年的同比基数。
据 TrendForc 统计，2026 年第三、 第四季度一般型 DRAM 合约价格环比分别上涨 13%—
18%和 10%—15%，涨幅较第一、 二季度的90%—95%和 58%—63%明显收窄。即使 2027
年部分存储产品价格继续维持高位， 价格同比涨幅也可能收窄， 对中国AI 产业链出口金

额增长的拉动相应减弱。
总体来看，2027 年全球 AI 需求仍有较强支撑， 中国AI 产业链相关商品出口有望保
持增长，但增速可能较 2026 年高位回落，叠加整体出口高基数及外部贸易摩擦等因素，
预计中国整体出口增速回落至 8%—10%。

股票投资评级说明 
投资评级分为股票投资评级和行业投资评级。

股票投资评级标准 
报告发布日后的 12 个月内公司股价的涨跌幅度相对同期沪深 300 指数的涨跌幅为基准，投资建议的评级标准为： 
买入：相对大盘涨幅大于 10%； 
增持：相对大盘涨幅在 5%~10%之间； 
持有：相对大盘涨幅在-5%~5%之间； 
减持：相对大盘涨幅小于-5%。

行业投资评级标准 
报告发布日后的 12 个月内行业股票指数的涨跌幅度相对同期沪深 300 指数的涨跌幅为基准，投资建议的评级标准为： 
增持：我们预计未来报告期内，行业整体回报高于基准指数 5%以上； 
中性：我们预计未来报告期内，行业整体回报介于基准指数-5%与 5%之间； 
减持：我们预计未来报告期内，行业整体回报低于基准指数 5%以下。

Document 491: なぜ暗号資産はハッキングされてもなくならないのか ～巨額流出が続いても消えない需要と、投資家が負うリスク～

Source PDF: all report pdf/なぜ暗号資産はハッキングされてもなくならないのか ～巨額流出が続いても消えない需要と、投資家が負うリスク～.pdf

Date: 未知日期
